1.12
Conclusion and Outlook

In this chapter, we have presented a nonlinear response theory based on a recently-developed density-matrix formalism in the linear response regime
[
29
,
30
,
33
]
, whereby the density matrix is decomposed into disorder-averaged and fluctuating parts. By treating the diagonal and off-diagonal elements of the density matrix in band space on an equal footing, the full contribution of the disorder interaction can be taken into account in a systematic manner. And through the perturbative solution of the quantum Liouville equation in the presence of electrostatic and weak disorder potentials, we have shown how the combined actions of the electric field and random impurities give rise to a multitude of scattering channels in the quadratic response regime, resulting in a classification of carrier densities in terms of distinct physical processes.

Moreover, in addition to the previously known linear-response extrinsic velocity, several extrinsic velocities have been identified, which only appear in the nonlinear response of the system, revealing the rich structure of interband coherence effects in this transport regime. As an application of this theory, we have studied the quadratic response in a prototypical model of tilted Dirac fermions, and have derived the leading and subleading disorder contributions to the nonlinear conductivities.

This suggests several follow-up studies. One possible direction would be to extend the analysis to nonlinear spin currents, which would allow for a detailed classification of spin-orbit torques in the nonlinear response regime. Another direction would be to apply the theory to more complicated disorder profiles, which have recently been shown to play a role in generating nonlinear responses
[
100
,
7
,
105
]
. Yet another possible direction would be to explore the zeroth-order contributions in specific materials–as has recently been initiated through other approaches as well
[
140
,
150
]
–in order to develop a better understanding of the contributions of the quadratic-response extrinsic velocities and their interplay with quantum geometry in this transport regime.

In closing, we note some physically relevant limits that can be relaxed in future studies based on the present approach. First, we have focussed our analysis on DC responses. Generalizing this to the AC regime, which is particularly significant in the study of nonlinear optical responses, will allow for the continued exploration of the resonance structures of nonlinear responses. This can have interesting signatures in quantum materials
[
38
]
and can be utilized as useful probes of quantum geometry
[
39
]
. In addition, in this initial study, we have limited our analysis to low temperatures. Relaxing this enables further analysis of finite-temperature effects via the quantum kinetic approach, including the effects of thermal magnons and nonlinear thermoelectric responses
[
43
]
. Finally, while it is customary to include the effect of a magnetic field in semiclassical transport studies of spin-orbit-coupled materials via the Zeeman interaction–similar to the exchange coupling in magnetic systems, a thorough analysis would also require the inclusion of the magnetic field in the derivation of the equations of motion. This leads to additional contributions, such as the coupling to the Berry curvature and modifying the density of states, which can then manifest in the nonlinear response of the system
[
146
]
.

Chapter 2
Quantum Response Theory and Momentum-Space Gravity

2.1
Introduction

The space of physical states of a quantum mechanical system–complex projective Hilbert space–has the structure of a Kahler manifold
[
151
]
. This has inspired various geometric and topological interpretations of physical phenomena in quantum systems over the years
[
152
,
37
]
. And, in several areas of exploration, this quantum state geometry has recently garnered immense interest
[
153
,
130
,
154
,
155
,
10
,
156
,
157
]
, with the identification of the role of the quantum metric
[
143
]
–the real part of the quantum geometric tensor (QGT)–in an increasing number of physical effects
[
158
,
159
,
160
,
161
,
119
,
131
,
39
,
123
,
124
,
147
,
126
,
162
,
163
,
164
,
165
,
166
]
.

While the dynamical significance of the Berry curvature has been known for decades
[
22
,
23
,
45
,
46
,
47
,
48
]
, it is perhaps the emergence of the quantum-metric Levi-Civita connection
[
146
]
that truly reveals the geometric underpinning of carrier dynamics and highlights the geodesic nature of the motion of Bloch electrons. Combining this with the Einstein field equations (EFE) arising from the Riemannian structure of quantum state manifolds has led to the intriguing recent proposal of momentum-space gravity
[
167
]
in condensed matter systems.

In this framework, the quantum metric in momentum space is viewed as the dual of the classical spacetime metric and the intrinsic dynamics is described by a dual Lorentz force. Furthermore, for mixed states, the momentum-space EFE acquire a source term that depends on the von Neumann entropy, which reveals intriguing similarities with efforts in understanding the fundamental nature of gravity and spacetime
[
168
,
169
,
170
,
171
,
172
,
173
,
174
,
175
]
and suggests a materials setting for exploring the connections between gravity and thermodynamics. Given the semiclassical nature of this approach based on wavepacket dynamics, a natural question that arises is the form this theory of gravity takes in momentum space from the vantage point of quantum response theory.

Here
[
11
]
, we address this question through a diagrammatic approach, with the general idea underlying this chapter presented schematically in Fig.
2.1
. We first apply Kubo formulas to propose a diagrammatic generalization of carrier dynamics to multiband systems in the presence of finite dissipation. To this end, we build on recent developments
[
176
,
177
,
178
,
179
,
180
,
181
]
in two-state
1
1

1

Here, we refer to an
n
-state quantum geometric object as one that carries
n
band indices. For example,
g_{\mu\nu}^{a}
and
g_{\mu\nu}^{ab}
are the single- and two-state quantum metric tensors, respectively.
quantum geometry to propose the
three-state
QGT and show that it appears already at the quadratic-response level. We also discuss the appearance of the quantum geometric
contorsion
tensor
[
151
]
and analogous symplectic terms when the full Berry covariant derivative is used to derive the Hermitian connection components.

Physically, much intuitive insight has been gained from viewing the Berry curvature as an intrinsic gauge field strength tensor in momentum space
[
37
]
. Following this, and the standard argument in general relativity
[
182
,
183
]
, we argue that once the choice of connection is made to be Levi-Civita, the remaining terms from the Hermitian connection and three-state QGT in the carrier position’s equation of motion can then be interpreted as intrinsic matter fields arising from the multistate quantum geometry.

(a)

Figure 2.1:
Schematic summary of this chapter. Correlation functions corresponding to conductivity Kubo formulas are applied to simultaneously dress with dissipation the quantum geometry, carrier dynamics and momentum-space field equations. The latter two yield dressed versions of the two central equations of general relativity, resulting in dressed gravity in momentum space.

A noteworthy feature of the present approach is the appearance of dressed quantum geometric quantities in the equations of motion and EFE. While we discuss the gravitational significance of this later on, here we mention that there is increasing ongoing effort to define quantum geometry in interacting and disordered systems
[
184
,
185
,
186
,
187
,
188
,
189
,
190
]
. The approach which naturally emerges in the present derivation is closest to that of Ref.
[
185
]
and, with the gravitational context in mind, is essentially a dissipation-induced multiband Weyl transformation of the quantum geometry. We apply this result to show that the dissipative correction to the EFE can be related to the entropic cost of dressing the metric through a dual momentum-space
drag force
induced by the dissipative scattering. This, in turn, can be thought of as providing a diagrammatic generalization of the corresponding semiclassical arguments to dissipative multiband systems. We conclude by presenting an outlook on possible insights that may be obtained from these results.

2.2
Multistate quantum geometry

In the presence of an applied electric field, the electric dipole interaction
e\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{r}
is included to the system Hamiltonian, where
\mathbf{r}
is the position operator. In the k-space representation, this takes the form
[
145
]

\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=-i\bm{\partial}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{k}}+\delta_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{k}}\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbf{k}}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{\mu\mathbf{k}}^{ab}=i\braket{u_{\mathbf{k}}^{a}|\partial_{\mu}^{\mathbf{k}}u_{\mathbf{k}}^{b}}
is the Berry connection and
\ket{u_{\mathbf{k}}^{a}}
the periodic part of the Bloch state
2
2

2

Regarding notation, we use Greek letters for real- or momentum-space indices–with Einstein summation implied–and Latin letters for band indices. Furthermore,
\mathcal{O}_{(\mu\nu)}\equiv(\mathcal{O}_{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{O}_{\nu\mu})/2
and
\mathcal{O}_{[\mu\nu]}\equiv(\mathcal{O}_{\mu\nu}-\mathcal{O}_{\nu\mu})/2
.
. We henceforth work locally in momentum space and thus drop the momentum parameter from the notation. The commutator of the position operator and local momentum-space operators naturally defines a Berry covariant derivative

\bm{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{O}\equiv-i[\mathbf{r},\mathcal{O}]
, which takes the form

(\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\mathcal{O})^{ab}=\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{O}^{ab}-i\left[\mathcal{A}_{\mu},\mathcal{O}\right]^{ab}.

(2.1)

It should be stressed that this includes the full Berry connection, which is decomposed into diagonal and off-diagonal elements in band space as

\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{ab}=a_{\mu}^{a}\delta^{ab}+\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}
.

The transition dipole matrix element
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}
can be viewed as a component of the complex-valued vielbein matrix
e_{\mu}^{ab}=\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\ket{u^{a}}\bra{u^{b}}
in band space
[
176
]
, thereby providing a geometric rationale for using off-diagonal Berry connection components as fundamental elements in the construction of multistate quantum geometric quantities. And the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product

\braket{A,B}=\text{Tr}(A^{\dagger}B)

induces complex Riemannian structure on the manifold of quantum states and allows for the definition of quantum geometric invariants. The simplest such two-state object is the well-known QGT, which is obtained from the inner product of tangent basis vectors,

Q_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\equiv\braket{e_{\nu}^{ab},e_{\mu}^{ab}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ba}
, and which obeys the projector calculus identity

i^{2}\bra{u^{a}}P^{a}\partial_{\mu}P^{b}\partial_{\nu}P^{a}\ket{u^{a}}=Q_{\mu\nu}^{ab}-\delta^{ab}\sum_{c}Q_{\mu\nu}^{ac},

(2.2)

with the projection operator given by

P^{a}=\ket{u^{a}}\bra{u^{a}}
. Note that for
b\neq a
, Eq. (
2.2
) may be regarded as an alternative definition of the QGT in the projector calculus language. Indeed, this approach can be used to identify other elements of two-state quantum geometry
[
179
,
178
]
, with the two-state QGT being the primary member. The real and imaginary parts of this tensor define the two-state quantum metric and Berry curvature tensors,

g_{\mu\nu}^{ab}=\text{Re}(Q_{\mu\nu}^{ab})
and

\Omega_{\mu\nu}^{ab}=-2\text{Im}(Q_{\mu\nu}^{ab})
. Furthermore, we note that the two-state QGT is basically the band resolution of single-state quantum geometry, and yields the familiar Fubini-Study metric and (single-state) Berry curvature tensors once one sums over intermediate states, as is done in the second term on the right in Eq. (
2.2
).

Moving beyond the QGT, the position operator acting on the tangent basis vectors induces a Hermitian connection
[
176
]
, the components of which are given by

C_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}=\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(\mathcal{D}_{\nu}\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}})^{ba}
. Using the standard definition of the (two-state) torsion tensor,

\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}=2C_{\mu[\nu\rho]}^{ba}
, we note that the real part of the Hermitian connection is expressed as

\text{Re}\left(C_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}\right)=\Gamma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}+\text{Re}\left(K_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}\right),

(2.3)

where

\Gamma_{\nu\rho\mu}^{ba}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu\rho}^{ba}+\partial_{\rho}g_{\mu\nu}^{ba}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\mu\rho}^{ba})

is a Levi-Civita connection component of the band-resolved quantum metric and the second term, given by

K_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}-\mathcal{T}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba}-\mathcal{T}_{\rho\mu\nu}^{ba}\right),

(2.4)

is identified as the quantum geometric contorsion tensor
[
151
]
, which contains the torsionful part of the metric connection and arises once one uses the full Berry covariant derivative to define the Hermitian connection, as opposed to using only the diagonal Berry connection components. We note that the contorsion tensor inherits the antisymmetric property

K_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}=K_{[\mu\nu]\rho}^{ba}
from the torsion tensor.

Motivated by a geometric characterization of nonlinear responses, we now present an extension of this framework beyond the two-state formalism by introducing the three-state QGT

Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}=\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}bc}\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}ca},

(2.5)

which cyclically connects three distinct states, and represents a natural generalization of its two-state counterpart. Formally, this can be defined by utilizing the non-Abelian QGT
[
143
,
191
,
177
,
181
]
in operator form, composed of basis vector derivatives, as

\mathcal{Q}_{\rho\nu}^{c}=\ket{\partial_{\rho}u^{c}}\bra{\partial_{\nu}u^{c}}
, which allows for the definition

Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}\equiv\Braket{\mathcal{Q}_{\rho\nu}^{c},e_{\mu}^{ab}},

(2.6)

with

\mathcal{(Q}_{\nu\rho}^{c})^{ba}=\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}bc}\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}ca}
. This naturally generalizes the two-state projector identity given by Eq. (
2.2
) to the three-state identity

\begin{split}i^{3}\bra{u^{a}}P^{a}\partial_{\mu}P^{b}\partial_{\nu}P^{c}\partial_{\rho}P^{a}\ket{u^{a}}=Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}-\delta^{ab}\sum_{d}Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{adc}+\delta^{ab}\sum_{d}Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{acd}-\delta^{bc}\sum_{d}Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abd},\end{split}

(2.7)

lending further support to the proposition that the three-state QGT defined above is indeed a central object in higher-state quantum geometry.

We point out that the real and imaginary parts of the three-state QGT are decomposed into linear combinations of the non-Abelian quantum metric and Berry curvature tensors as

\displaystyle\text{Re}\left(Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}(ab)}(\textbf{g}_{\nu\rho}^{c})^{ba}-\frac{i}{2}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}[ab]}(\bm{\Omega}_{\nu\rho}^{c})^{ba},

(2.8a)

\displaystyle\text{Im}\left(Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}\right)

\displaystyle=-i\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}[ab]}(\textbf{g}_{\nu\rho}^{c})^{ba}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}(ab)}(\bm{\Omega}_{\nu\rho}^{c})^{ba},

(2.8b)

thereby exhibiting a more complex structure compared to the two-state counterpart. The symmetric and antisymmetric parts,

S_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}=Q_{\mu(\nu\rho)}^{abc}

and

A_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}=Q_{\mu[\nu\rho]}^{abc}
, are also expressible in terms of these quantities. Specifically, we note that the antisymmetric term is the band resolution of the torsion tensor, i.e.,

\sum_{c}A_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}=-i\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}/2
, while

S_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}
is its symmetric counterpart. As is shown in the next section, the three-state QGT already makes an appearance at the quadratic-response level in dissipative multiband systems and is required for a complete geometric characterization of the response functions.

2.3
Dressed carrier dynamics from diagrammatics

Motivated by recent developments via density-matrix methods in generalizing carrier dynamics
[
33
,
9
]
, here, we obtain the equation of motion for the carrier position by taking the route of Kubo formulas. Using recently developed diagrammatic approaches within the Matsubara formalism
[
132
,
192
]
, the linear and quadratic ac conductivities are obtained. In order to obtain dissipative carrier dynamics, we note that the dc current density,

j_{\mu}=\sigma_{\mu\nu}E^{\nu}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}E^{\nu}E^{\rho}
, is related to the equation of motion of the carrier position and the carrier density
n
as

j_{\mu}=-e\text{Tr}(n\dot{x}_{\mu})

[
33
,
9
]
. Therefore, upon evaluating the correlation functions, and using the equation of motion of the carrier momentum,

\dot{k}^{\mu}=-eE^{\mu}/\hbar
,
we arrive at the equation of motion for the carrier position, which is expressed as

\begin{split}\dot{x}_{\mu}^{a}&=v_{\mu}^{a}+\dot{k}^{\nu}\sum_{b}(\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega}^{ab}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}-\mathcal{Z}_{g}^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ba}+\dot{k}^{\rho}m^{ab}\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}^{ab}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{ba})\\
&+\dot{k}^{\nu}\dot{k}^{\rho}\sum_{b}(\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ba}\partial_{\mu}+\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}+\sum_{c}\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})m^{ab},\end{split}

(2.9)

where
v_{\mu}^{a}=\partial_{\mu}\varepsilon^{a}/\hbar
is the group velocity and the
\mathcal{Z}
’s are various renormalization factors that depend on the dissipation parameter. Eq. (
2.9
) is one of the central results of this chapter and represents a diagrammatic generalization of carrier dynamics to dissipative multiband systems. As can be seen, in addition to dressing the known terms related to the Berry curvature and Levi-Civita connection, which are interpreted as the (multiband) momentum-space magnetic field and geodesic contributions, respectively, several additional contributions are identified. The remainder of this section is devoted to a discussion of these terms and their physical significance, particularly within the context of the gravitational interpretation.

The matrix
m^{ab}=\hbar/\varepsilon^{ab}
(with

\varepsilon^{ab}\equiv\varepsilon^{a}-\varepsilon^{b}
) couples to the Christoffel symbol in the equation of motion and may thus be regarded as the local multiband generalization of the effective mass term in Ref.
[
167
]
. Physically, this implies that the effective gravitational force the electron in band
a
experiences from the Levi-Civita connection between bands
b
and
a
is inversely proportional to the local energy difference between the two bands. Interestingly, this force can be attractive or repulsive depending on the sign of
\varepsilon^{ab}
. And since the effective mass is a local quantity, its variation also contributes to the equation of motion, which explains the

\partial_{\mu}m^{ab}
term in the equation of motion.

It is worth elaborating further on the appearance of dressed quantum geometric quantities in Eq. (
2.9
) as opposed to bare ones. With the exception of the Levi-Civita connection, these are defined as

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{ab}\equiv\lambda^{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}
,
where

\lambda^{ab}=[1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}]^{-1}
,
and
\eta^{ab}=\gamma/\varepsilon^{ab}
is a dimensionless measure of the dissipation strength, with
\gamma
the self-energy
3
3

3

We also note that this choice of dressing has the favorable property of preserving the
a\leftrightarrow b
symmetries of the quantum metric and Berry curvature in band space.
. We stress that dressing the quantum geometry is an essential inclusion for a gravitational interpretation to hold, as, in general relativity, the matter distribution and the (spacetime) curvature are related and influence each other
[
182
]
.

In our case, the introduction of scatterers to the system has the effect of screening the quantum geometry through the interband scattering function
\lambda^{ab}
, which acts as a dissipation-induced multiband Weyl transformation on the various geometric terms in the equation of motion. Physically, this accounts for the spectral broadening of the electron propagator from self-energy corrections due to scattering, and results in an effective rescaling of the two-state QGT in the linear response function. And the remaining dissipative corrections that arise in the quadratic responses can then be naturally absorbed in the renormalization functions, implying that the local ratios of the momentum-space forces an electron in a given band experiences from the various geometric terms are also affected by scattering, reflecting the nontrivial modification of the dynamics by dissipation.

The additional quantum geometric objects that appear in Eq. (
2.9
) are given by

\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}=\mathcal{Z}_{K}^{ab}\text{Re}(\tilde{K}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})+\mathcal{Z}_{C}^{ab}\text{Im}\left(\tilde{C}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\rho\nu\mu}^{ba}\right),

(2.10)

which contains the contribution from the rest of the dressed Hermitian connection–including the contorsion–and

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}&=\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{abc}\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{2}^{abc}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{3}^{abc}\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\\
&+\mathcal{Z}_{4}^{abc}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{5}^{abc}\text{Re}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{6}^{abc}\text{Im}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc}),\end{split}

(2.11)

which encapsulates the contributions from the dressed three-state QGT
4
4

4

Note that we define the dressed three-state QGT in terms of the first two band indices as
\tilde{Q}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}=Q_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}/1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}
. Together,
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
can be intuitively viewed as describing intrinsic momentum-space matter fields
[
182
,
183
]
that help capture the quantum geometry of Bloch electrons, including the symplectic and non-Abelian contributions, at second order in the carrier momentum.

Finally, consider now the term proportional to
\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ba}
in Eq. (
2.9
), which includes a dissipative linear-response addition to the equation of motion

\dot{x}_{\mu}^{a}\supset-2\dot{k}^{\nu}\sum_{b}\eta^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ba}

(2.12)

that is also not captured in the semiclassical or density-matrix approaches. Applying the dual-space transformation
\mathbf{x}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{k}
as discussed in Ref.
[
167
]
to this term reveals that, if the geodesic term is to be regarded as the momentum-space gravitational force, then the contribution from Eq. (
2.12
) can be thought of as arising from a dual drag force in momentum space induced by the scattering. In the next section, we discuss this more within the context of the EFE.

2.4
Dissipative Einstein field equations

We now discuss the effect of dissipation on the multiband momentum-space EFE and how it sources the field equations within the present formalism. In the absence of dissipation, the quantum metric is Fubini-Study, which is the canonical Riemannian metric of complex projective Hilbert space. This is an Einstein metric, with the Ricci tensor being proportional to the metric. Therefore, the bare metric satisfies the sourceless EFE, corresponding to the vacuum solution of the gravitational theory. Consider next the dissipative case. For convenience, we initially drop band indices from the notation, such that the indices
ab
are implied for the various quantities that appear (i.e.,

g_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow g_{\mu\nu}^{ab},R_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow R_{\mu\nu}^{ab}
, etc.). The inverse of the dressed metric is identified as

\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\lambda^{-1}g^{\mu\nu}
, such that

\tilde{g}^{\mu\rho}\tilde{g}_{\rho\nu}=g^{\mu\rho}g_{\rho\nu}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}
, i.e., the dressed (bare) metric raises and lowers indices in the presence (absence) of dissipation. And the screened Levi-Civita connection is defined in the usual sense
[
183
]
,

\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{g}=0
, which implies that the dressed Christoffel symbol is that of the dressed metric

\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mu\nu\rho}=\lambda\Gamma_{\mu\nu\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\left(g_{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\rho}\lambda+g_{\mu\rho}\nabla_{\nu}\lambda-g_{\nu\rho}\nabla_{\mu}\lambda\right).

(2.13)

This can then be used to derive the dressed Riemann tensor

\mathchoice{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}

and its contractions, namely the Ricci tensor

\tilde{R}_{\mu\nu}=\mathchoice{\tilde{R}^{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}{\rho}{\nu}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}{\rho}{\nu}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.420 and Ricci scalar

\tilde{R}=\mathchoice{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\tilde{R}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}
.
Combining terms, we arrive at the dissipative EFE

\tilde{R}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{R}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu},

(2.14)

where
\Lambda
and
T_{\mu\nu}
, which emerge entirely as a result of dissipative scattering, are identified as the local momentum-space cosmological constant and stress tensor, respectively, and are given, up to
O(\eta^{2})
, by

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\nabla^{2}\eta^{2},

(2.15a)

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-2}{2}\left(\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}\right)\eta^{2},

(2.15b)

with
n
the dimension of the system. This assignment is rather arbitrary as far as the EFE is concerned, and
\Lambda
may well be absorbed into the stress tensor
[
182
]
. What matters here instead is that the dressed Einstein tensor

\tilde{G}_{\mu\nu}=\tilde{R}_{\mu\nu}-\tilde{R}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}/2
does not vanish, which physically implies that dissipation is providing a source term for the momentum-space EFE.

To gain further insight into this result, it is helpful to recall that the modification of the EFE emerged from the screening of the metric. Following the semiclassical results, one could ask whether there is an entropic explanation for this. To answer this in a consistent manner within the present response-theory formalism, we note that, to second order in the electric field, the rate of local entropy production by microscopic scattering processes is determined by the symmetric part of the linear conductivity tensor
[
193
]

\dot{S}=\beta\mathbf{j}\cdot\mathbf{E}=\beta\sigma_{(\mu\nu)}E^{\mu}E^{\nu},

(2.16)

which is related to Joule heating and has a quantum geometric contribution,
\dot{S}_{\text{g}}
, identified as giving rise to the momentum-space drag force, Eq. (
2.12
). This corresponds to the local conductivity contribution

-2e^{2}/\hbar\sum_{ab}f^{a}\eta^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}

upon restoring band indices. Introducing the band resolution of the entropy rate as

\dot{S}=\sum_{ab}\dot{S}^{ab}
, to
O(\eta^{2})
we find

\dot{S}_{\text{g}}^{ab}=-2\hbar\beta\dot{k}^{\mu}\dot{k}^{\nu}f^{a}g_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\eta^{ab}.

(2.17)

This demonstrates that the source term in the multiband EFE can be reexpressed as a function of the local entropy rate associated with dissipative interband processes, thereby providing a quantum-response generalization of the semiclassical arguments to dissipative multiband systems. This demonstrates that the source term in the multiband EFE can be reexpressed as a function of the local entropy rate associated with dissipative interband processes, thereby providing a quantum-response generalization of the arguments in Ref.
[
167
]
to dissipative multiband systems. Furthermore, in this sense, one can think of momentum-space gravity as essentially being a manifestation of local entropy changes in momentum-space, which is reminiscent of similar insights on gravity in spacetime. In this light, one could also think of the quantum geometric drag force related to Eq. (
2.12
) as a counterpart of the entropic force discussed in Ref.
[
173
]
, with the difference that the latter–inspired by holographic arguments–is typically not associated with a fundamental field. In our case, this role is played by the quantum metric itself.

2.5
Geometric origin of dissipative responses and dynamics

In this section, after elaborating on the basic diagrammatic framework for evaluating conductivities in the presence of finite dissipation, we take the dc limit and derive the equation of motion that arises from the response functions. In doing so, we also show how both linear and nonlinear responses can be fully reexpressed in the geometric language, which, to our knowledge, has not been demonstrated before. Indeed, while there are several geometric presentations of
specific
quadratic responses in the literature, a complete geometric classification of quadratic response theory that takes into account all Feynman diagrams simultaneously is yet to be done. In the derivations of the nonlinear responses presented below, several known elements of two-state quantum geometry make an appearance, including the quantum metric, Berry curvature and quantum connection. Furthermore, while two-state quantum geometry is certainly a necessary element, we find that it is not sufficient, as there are terms in the quadratic response functions which do not take closed two-state forms. This should perhaps not come as a surprise, as it would seem reasonable to question the complete classification of the three-point functions of quadratic response theory (involving traces over three distinct states) via two-state objects. As it turns out, we find that it is precisely the three-state QGT that is the missing necessary ingredient to complete the geometric classification.

2.5.1
Response functions and frequency summations

Using a diagrammatic approach within the Matsubara formalism
[
132
]
, and applying the Feynman rules presented in Fig.
2.2
, the linear and quadratic ac conductivities are obtained as

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu}(\omega;\omega^{{}^{\prime}})=&\frac{ie^{2}}{\hbar\omega^{{}^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\text{Tr}\left[h_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n})h_{\nu}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}})+h_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n})\right],\end{split}

(2.18)

and

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}(\omega;\omega^{{}^{\prime}},\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})=&\frac{e}{2\hbar}\frac{(ie)^{2}}{\omega^{{}^{\prime}}\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\text{Tr}\left[h_{\mu\rho}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n})h_{\nu}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}})+\frac{1}{2}h_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n})h_{\nu\rho}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}}+\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})\right.\\
&+\left.\frac{1}{2}h_{\mu\nu\rho}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n})+h_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n})h_{\nu}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}})h_{\rho}\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}}+\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})\right.\\
&+\left.\{(\nu,\omega^{{}^{\prime}})\leftrightarrow(\rho,\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})\}\right],\end{split}

(2.19)

where
\omega
is the frequency of the output photon and
\omega^{{}^{\prime}},\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
are input frequencies. The Matsubara Green’s function is given by

\mathcal{G}(\omega_{n})=(i\omega_{n}-H-\Sigma)^{-1}
, where
H
is the system Hamiltonian, with

H\ket{\psi^{a}}=\varepsilon^{a}\ket{\psi^{a}}
. And for the self-energy, we make the phenomenelogical approximation
\Sigma=-i\gamma/2
, with
\gamma
measuring the strength of dissipation induced by scattering events. Finally, velocity operators are obtained by applying successive Berry covariant derivatives,

h_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}=\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}H
.

(a)

Figure 2.2:
Linear [(a) and (b)] and quadratic [(c)-(f)] conductivity diagrams. Loops and legs are electron and photon propagators, respectively, and vertices imply velocity operator insertions, with the index
\mu
reserved for the output. The Feynman rules resulting in Eqs. (
2.18
) and (
2.19
) consist of the following factors and procedures: 1) A factor of
1/k!
for every set of
k
connected photons. 2) Factors of
e/\hbar
for the output photon and
ie/\chi
for each input photon, with
\chi=\omega^{{}^{\prime}},\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
. 3) Trace over momentum and band indices of the loop. 4) Matsubara frequency summation
1/\beta\sum_{n}
.

To evaluate Eqs. (
2.18
) and (
2.19
), the effect of the dissipation parameter
\gamma
is included through a phenomenological shift of the frequency factors in the complex plane,

\omega\rightarrow\omega+i\gamma

[
132
]
. And the Matsubara Green’s function is expressed in the spectral representation as

\mathcal{G}^{a}(\omega_{n})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\varepsilon}{2\pi}\frac{A^{a}(\varepsilon)}{i\omega_{n}-\varepsilon},

(2.20)

where the denominator is the bare Green’s function,

\mathcal{G}_{0}^{a}(\omega_{n})=(i\omega_{n}-\varepsilon^{a})^{-1}
and

A^{a}(\varepsilon)=-2\text{Im}G_{R}^{a}(\varepsilon)

is the spectral function
[
34
]
, with
G_{R}
the retarded Green’s function. The frequency summations that are relevant to the evaluation of the conductivities are given by
[
34
,
192
]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\mathcal{G}_{0}^{a}(\omega_{n})

\displaystyle=f^{a},

(2.21a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\mathcal{G}_{0}^{a}(\omega_{n})\mathcal{G}_{0}^{b}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{\omega^{{}^{\prime}}+\varepsilon^{ab}},

(2.21b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\mathcal{G}_{0}^{a}(\omega_{n})\mathcal{G}_{0}^{b}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}})\mathcal{G}_{0}^{c}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}}+\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=\frac{(\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}-\varepsilon^{cb})(f^{a}-f^{b})+(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}-\varepsilon^{ba})(f^{c}-f^{b})}{(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}-\varepsilon^{ba})(\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}-\varepsilon^{cb})(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}+\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}-\varepsilon^{ca})},

(2.21c)

with the shorthand
f^{a}\equiv f(\varepsilon^{a})
for the Fermi distribution. In addition, an approximation is needed to obtain analytical results in the presence of dissipation. Taking

\gamma\beta\ll 1

results in the identity
[
192
]

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\varepsilon}{2\pi}A^{a}(\varepsilon)F\left(\varepsilon,\{\omega_{n}\}\right)f(\varepsilon)\simeq F\left(\varepsilon^{a}\pm\frac{i\gamma}{2},\{\omega_{n}\}\right)f(\varepsilon^{a}\pm\frac{i\gamma}{2}),

(2.22)

where
F
is a general function that includes velocity operator components and the sign is chosen
\pm
when
F
is analytic in the upper/lower complex plane of the energy integrand. We note that this approximation is not as restricting as may seem, as values as large as
\gamma\beta=0.5
have been shown to yield good agreement with numerical results as far as the conductivity is concerned
[
192
]
. We apply these steps to Eqs. (
2.18
) and (
2.19
) and take the dc limit

\omega,\omega^{{}^{\prime}},\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\rightarrow 0
, yielding the frequency summations

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\mathcal{G}^{a}(\omega_{n})\mathcal{G}^{b}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}})\rightarrow\frac{f(\varepsilon^{a}+\frac{i\gamma}{2})-f(\varepsilon^{b}-\frac{i\gamma}{2})}{\varepsilon^{ab}+i\gamma}\simeq\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{\varepsilon^{ab}+i\gamma}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}\left(\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}+\frac{\partial f^{b}}{\partial\varepsilon^{b}}\right)\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{ab}+i\gamma},

(2.23a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\mathcal{G}^{a}(\omega_{n})\mathcal{G}^{b}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}})\mathcal{G}^{c}(\omega_{n}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}}+\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})\rightarrow\frac{f(\varepsilon^{a}+\frac{i\gamma}{2})-f(\varepsilon^{b}-\frac{i\gamma}{2})}{(\varepsilon^{ba}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ca}-i\gamma)}+\frac{f(\varepsilon^{c}-\frac{i\gamma}{2})-f(\varepsilon^{b}+\frac{i\gamma}{2})}{(\varepsilon^{cb}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ca}-i\gamma)}

(2.23b)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{(\varepsilon^{ba}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ca}-i\gamma)}+\frac{f^{c}-f^{b}}{(\varepsilon^{cb}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ca}-i\gamma)}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}\left(\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}+\frac{\partial f^{b}}{\partial\varepsilon^{b}}\right)\frac{1}{(\varepsilon^{ba}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ca}-i\gamma)}

(2.23c)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;-i\frac{\gamma}{2}\left(\frac{\partial f^{c}}{\partial\varepsilon^{c}}+\frac{\partial f^{b}}{\partial\varepsilon^{b}}\right)\frac{1}{(\varepsilon^{cb}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ca}-i\gamma)}.

(2.23d)

2.5.2
DC conductivities and dressed equation of motion

Using the explanations of the previous section, the dc response functions can be evaluated. From Eq. (
2.23
), it is clear that both the distribution function and its derivatives appear in the responses. As is shown below, for the purpose of deriving the equation of motion at nonlinear order, it is necessary to include the latter terms in order to obtain the correct signs of the carrier densities in the nonlinear response functions.

The linear dc conductivity reads

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu}&=\frac{e^{2}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}\left(f^{a}h_{\mu\nu}^{a}+\sum_{b}\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{\varepsilon^{ab}+i\gamma}h_{\nu}^{ab}h_{\mu}^{ba}\right)\\
&=\frac{e^{2}}{\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}f^{a}\partial_{\nu}v_{\mu}^{a}+\frac{ie^{2}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}(f^{a}-f^{b})\frac{Q_{\mu\nu}^{ab}}{1-i\eta^{ab}}\\
&=-\frac{e^{2}}{\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}\partial_{\nu}f^{a}v_{\mu}^{a}+\frac{e^{2}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}-\frac{2e^{2}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\eta^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab},\end{split}

(2.24)

yielding the linear current density

\frac{j_{\mu}^{1}}{-e}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}n_{1}^{a}v_{\mu}^{a}+\dot{k}^{\nu}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}-2\dot{k}^{\nu}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\eta^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab},

(2.25)

with the linear carrier density given by
n_{1}^{a}=-\tau\dot{k}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}f^{a}
and
\tau=\hbar/\gamma
the relaxation time. Note that Eq. (
2.24
) reveals the natural choice of dressing for the quantum geometric quantities in the sense that the Weyl-transformed Berry curvature and quantum metric represent the effective geometry the carriers experience in the presence of dissipation within the relaxation time approximation. This is then consistently applied in the evaluation of the nonlinear responses as well, the calculation of which is considerably more detailed. However, the contributions can be systematically analyzed and classified as follows. Taking into account the four terms in Eq. (
2.19
), we decompose the nonlinear conductivity tensor as

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{A}}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{B}},

(2.26a)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{A}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{I,A}}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{II,A}}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{III,A}}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{IV,A}},

(2.26b)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{B}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{I,B}}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{II,B}}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{III,B}}+\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{IV,B}},

(2.26c)

where the labels “A” and “B” specify Fermi distribution terms and Fermi distribution derivative terms, respectively, as per Eq. (
2.23
). The former terms yield the contributions

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{I,A}}&=\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}f^{a}h_{\mu\nu\rho}^{a}+(\nu\leftrightarrow\rho)=\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}f^{a}\left[\hbar\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}v_{\mu}^{a}-2\sum_{b}\partial_{\mu}(g_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\varepsilon^{ab})\right]\\
&-\frac{ie^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}(f^{a}-f^{b})\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\left[-i\partial_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}ba}\varepsilon^{ab})-i\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ba}\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{ab}-\sum_{c}(\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{bc}\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{ca}\varepsilon^{ac}-\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{ca}\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{bc}\varepsilon^{cb})\right],\end{split}

(2.27)

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{II,A}}&=\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma}h_{\mu\rho}^{ab}h_{\nu}^{ba}+(\nu\leftrightarrow\rho)\\
&=\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma}\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ba}\varepsilon^{ab}\left[\partial_{\mu}(\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\varepsilon^{ab})+\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{ab}+i\sum_{c}(\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{ac}\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{cb}\varepsilon^{bc}-\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{ac}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{cb}\varepsilon^{ca})\right],\end{split}

(2.28)

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{III,A}}&=\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{\varepsilon^{ab}+i\gamma}h_{\nu\rho}^{ab}h_{\mu}^{ba}+(\nu\leftrightarrow\rho)\\
&=\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{f^{a}-f^{b}}{\varepsilon^{ab}+i\gamma}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ba}\varepsilon^{ab}\left[\partial_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\varepsilon^{ab})+\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{ab}+i\sum_{c}(\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{ac}\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{cb}\varepsilon^{bc}-\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{ac}\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{cb}\varepsilon^{ca})\right],\end{split}

(2.29)

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{IV,A}}=&\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\left[\frac{f^{a}-f^{c}}{(\varepsilon^{ac}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma)}+\frac{f^{b}-f^{c}}{(\varepsilon^{cb}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma)}\right]h_{\mu}^{ab}h_{\nu}^{bc}h_{\rho}^{ca}+(\nu\leftrightarrow\rho)\\
=&\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\left[\frac{f^{a}-f^{c}}{(\varepsilon^{ac}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma)}+\frac{f^{b}-f^{c}}{(\varepsilon^{cb}-i\gamma)(\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma)}\right]\\
&\times\left[Q_{\nu\rho}^{ac}\delta^{ab}(\varepsilon^{ac})^{2}\partial_{\mu}\varepsilon^{a}+Q_{\mu\rho}^{ab}\delta^{bc}(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}\partial_{\nu}\varepsilon^{b}+Q_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\delta^{ac}(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{a}-iQ_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc}\varepsilon^{ab}\varepsilon^{bc}\varepsilon^{ca}\right].\end{split}

(2.30)

Combining the four terms and after a little algebra, we arrive at the expression

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{A}}=&\frac{e^{3}}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}f^{a}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}v_{\mu}^{a}-\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\frac{\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{ab}}{\varepsilon^{ab}}\frac{1-3(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\partial_{\rho}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{ab}}\right)\frac{3-(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{ab}}\right)\frac{1-(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\mu}\varepsilon^{ab}-\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{ab}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{ab}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{F}_{i}\end{split}

(2.31)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{1}=&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\text{Im}\bigg\{\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(\mathcal{D}_{\nu}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right])^{ba}}{\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma}\bigg\}\\
=&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\frac{\varepsilon^{ab}}{(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{\mu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\nu}\varepsilon^{ab}+\text{Im}(C_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}-\sum_{c}\text{Re}(S_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})(\varepsilon^{bc}-\varepsilon^{ca})\right]\\
&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\frac{1}{(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}}\left[g_{\mu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\nu}\varepsilon^{ab}+\text{Re}(C_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}+\sum_{c}\text{Im}(S_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})(\varepsilon^{bc}-\varepsilon^{ca})\right],\end{split}

(2.32)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{2}=&\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\frac{\varepsilon^{ab}}{(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}}\text{Im}\{\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right])^{ba}\}\\
=&\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\frac{\varepsilon^{ab}}{(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}}\left[\text{Im}(C_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}-\sum_{c}\text{Re}(S_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})(\varepsilon^{bc}-\varepsilon^{ca})-\frac{1}{2}\text{Im}(\mathcal{T}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}\right],\end{split}

(2.33)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{3}=&\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\frac{\text{Im}\{\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}},\left[\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right]\right]^{ba}\}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\\
=&\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\left[\text{Re}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}+\sum_{c}\text{Im}(\tilde{Q}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\varepsilon^{bc}-\sum_{c}\text{Im}(\tilde{Q}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{abc})\varepsilon^{ca}\right],\end{split}

(2.34)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{4}=&\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}f^{a}\text{Im}\big\{\left(\left[\left[\alpha_{\mu}(\gamma),H\right],\left[\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right]\right]\left[\alpha_{\rho}(-\gamma),H\right]\right)_{\mathbf{k}}^{a}\big\}\\
=&\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}\bigg\{\text{Im}(Q_{\rho\nu\mu}^{abc})\frac{\varepsilon^{ac}\varepsilon^{ab}-\gamma^{2}}{[(\varepsilon^{ac})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}-\text{Re}(Q_{\rho\nu\mu}^{abc})\frac{\gamma(\varepsilon^{ac}+\varepsilon^{ab})}{[(\varepsilon^{ac})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}\\
&-\text{Im}(Q_{\rho\mu\nu}^{abc})\frac{\varepsilon^{cb}\varepsilon^{ab}-\gamma^{2}}{[(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{bc})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}+\text{Re}(Q_{\rho\mu\nu}^{abc})\frac{\gamma(\varepsilon^{cb}+\varepsilon^{ab})}{[(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{bc})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}\bigg\}\varepsilon^{ab}\varepsilon^{bc}\varepsilon^{ca},\end{split}

(2.35)

with
[\alpha_{\mu}(\gamma)]^{ab}\equiv\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}/(\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma)
. Reinserting these back into Eq. (
2.31
) and collecting similar geometric terms, the conductivity reads

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{A}=&\frac{e^{3}}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}v_{\mu}^{a}-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\partial_{\rho}m^{ab}\frac{2\eta^{ab}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\partial_{\rho}m^{ab}\frac{1-(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}m^{ab}-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}m^{ab}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{ab}+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\mu}m^{ab}\frac{1}{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\frac{1+2(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\\
&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}m^{ab}\text{Re}(\tilde{K}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}m^{ab}\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\text{Im}\left(\tilde{C}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\rho\nu\mu}^{ba}\right)\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}m^{ab}\left[\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})-\eta^{ab}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})\right]\left(\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}-\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}\right)\\
&+\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}m^{ab}\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&+\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}m^{ab}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&-\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}m^{ab}\text{Re}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&+\frac{2e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}m^{ab}\text{Im}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right],\end{split}

(2.36)

which reveals the various contributions from the dressed two-state quantum geometric objects. The remaining terms, which cannot be expressed within the two-state formalism, are precisely captured by the symmetric and antisymmetric parts of the three-state QGT. This highlights an interesting–and perhaps useful–parallel with the appearance of the two-state QGT in dissipative linear response theory. As can be seen from Eq. (
2.24
), the two-state QGT makes an appearance in the response function, which cannot be summed out in general. For example, focussing on the the Berry curvature term responsible for the anomalous Hall effect,

\dot{k}^{\nu}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}
,
we note that in the clean limit, one can sum out the intermediate states of the two-state Berry curvature, leaving behind the single-band Berry curvature. That is, in this limit, one could remove all indications that this term is essentially an (intrinsic) interband coherence effect and express it as a single-state object. However, this is no longer the case for the dressed Berry curvature and one should more appropriately consider its band resolution, as a result of the additional interband mixing arising from the dissipation. In this sense, the appearance of the three-state QGT can be regarded as the quadratic-response counterpart to the above argument.

We next repeat the above steps for the distribution derivative terms. This yields the conductivity contribution

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\text{B}}=&-\frac{3e^{3}}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}f^{a}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}v_{\mu}^{a}-\frac{e^{3}}{4\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{ab}\frac{1+5(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ab}+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\mu}\varepsilon^{ab}\varepsilon^{ab}\frac{1+2(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\\
&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\varepsilon^{ab}\left[1+3(\eta^{ab})^{2}\right]-\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\frac{\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{ab}}{\varepsilon^{ab}}\frac{1-3(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{K}_{i},\end{split}

(2.37)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{1}=&\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\varepsilon^{ab}\text{Im}\bigg\{\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(\mathcal{D}_{\nu}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right])^{ba}}{\varepsilon^{ab}-i\gamma}\bigg\}\\
=&\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\frac{1}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{\mu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\nu}\varepsilon^{ab}+\text{Im}(C_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}-\sum_{c}\text{Re}(S_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})(\varepsilon^{bc}-\varepsilon^{ca})\right]\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\frac{\varepsilon^{ab}}{(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}}\left[g_{\mu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\nu}\varepsilon^{ab}+\text{Re}(C_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}+\sum_{c}\text{Im}(S_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})(\varepsilon^{bc}-\varepsilon^{ca})\right],\end{split}

(2.38)

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{2}=&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\frac{\text{Im}\{\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right])^{ba}\}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\\
=&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\left[\text{Im}(\tilde{C}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}-\sum_{c}\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})(\varepsilon^{bc}-\varepsilon^{ca})-\frac{1}{2}\text{Im}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba})\varepsilon^{ab}\right],\end{split}

(2.39)

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{3}=&\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\frac{\varepsilon^{ab}}{(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}}\text{Im}\{\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}},\left[\mathcal{A}_{\rho}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right]\right]^{ba}\}\\
=&\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\left[\text{Re}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba})+\sum_{c}\text{Im}(\tilde{Q}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\frac{\varepsilon^{bc}}{\varepsilon^{ab}}-\sum_{c}\text{Im}(\tilde{Q}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{abc})\frac{\varepsilon^{ca}}{\varepsilon^{ab}}\right],\end{split}

(2.40)

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{4}=&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\text{Re}\big\{\left(\left[\left[\alpha_{\mu}(\gamma),H\right],\left[\mathcal{A}_{\nu}^{{}^{\prime}},H\right]\right]\left[\alpha_{\rho}(-\gamma),H\right]\right)_{\mathbf{k}}^{a}\big\}\\
=&\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\bigg\{\text{Re}(Q_{\rho\nu\mu}^{abc})\frac{\varepsilon^{ac}\varepsilon^{ab}-\gamma^{2}}{[(\varepsilon^{ac})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}+\text{Im}(Q_{\rho\nu\mu}^{abc})\frac{\gamma(\varepsilon^{ac}+\varepsilon^{ab})}{[(\varepsilon^{ac})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}\\
&-\text{Re}(Q_{\rho\mu\nu}^{abc})\frac{\varepsilon^{cb}\varepsilon^{ab}-\gamma^{2}}{[(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{bc})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}-\text{Im}(Q_{\rho\mu\nu}^{abc})\frac{\gamma(\varepsilon^{cb}+\varepsilon^{ab})}{[(\varepsilon^{ab})^{2}+\gamma^{2}][(\varepsilon^{bc})^{2}+\gamma^{2}]}\bigg\}\varepsilon^{ab}\varepsilon^{bc}\varepsilon^{ca},\end{split}

(2.41)

resulting in the contribution

\begin{split}\sigma_{\mu\nu\rho}^{B}=&-\frac{3e^{3}}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}v_{\mu}^{a}+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}-\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\partial_{\rho}\varepsilon^{ab}\frac{1+3(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}-\frac{3e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\eta^{ab}-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar\gamma^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\partial_{\rho}f^{a}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\varepsilon^{ab}\\
&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\varepsilon^{ab}\partial_{\rho}m^{ab}\frac{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}+\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{ab}\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ab}\varepsilon^{ab}\partial_{\mu}m^{ab}\left[\frac{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}-\frac{2}{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\frac{1+2(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\right]\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\text{Re}(\tilde{K}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})+\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\text{Im}\left(\tilde{C}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\rho\nu\mu}^{ba}\right)\\
&-\frac{e^{3}}{2\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\left[\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})-\eta^{ab}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})\right]\left(\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}-\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}\right)\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&+\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\text{Re}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\left[\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&-\frac{e^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\text{Im}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right].\end{split}

(2.42)

Adding all the various conductivity contributions, the total current density can be obtained. To do so, we note that the distribution derivative terms in Eq. (
2.42
) can be reexpressed with the identity

\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{\partial f^{a}}{\partial\varepsilon^{a}}\mathcal{O}=-\sum_{\mathbf{k}}f^{a}\partial^{\sigma}\left[\frac{1}{\hbar}\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\mathcal{O}\right]=-\sum_{\mathbf{k}}f^{a}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{1}{\hbar}\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\bigg\}\mathcal{O},

(2.43)

where
\mathcal{O}
is an arbitrary momentum- and band-dependent function and the curly brackets in the last term denote the anticommutator.

Collecting terms from the linear and nonlinear conductivities, we ultimately arrive at the total current density

\begin{split}\frac{j_{\mu}}{-e}=&\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}(n_{1}^{a}+n_{2}^{a})v_{\mu}^{a}+\dot{k}^{\nu}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}(f^{a}+n_{1}^{a})\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega}^{ab}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}-\dot{k}^{\nu}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}(f^{a}+n_{1}^{a})\mathcal{Z}_{g}^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}\\
&+\dot{k}^{\nu}\dot{k}^{\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}m^{ab}\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}^{ab}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{ba}+\dot{k}^{\nu}\dot{k}^{\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}m^{ab}\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}^{ab}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\nu\rho\mu}^{ba}+\dot{k}^{\nu}\dot{k}^{\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{ab}\tilde{g}_{\nu\rho}^{ab}\partial_{\mu}m^{ab}\\
&+\dot{k}^{\nu}\dot{k}^{\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{k}ab}f^{a}m^{ab}\left[\mathcal{Z}_{K}^{ab}\text{Re}(\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba})+\mathcal{Z}_{C}^{ab}\text{Im}\left(\tilde{C}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{ba}-\tilde{C}_{\rho\nu\mu}^{ba}\right)\right]\\
&+\dot{k}^{\nu}\dot{k}^{\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}m^{ab}\left[\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{abc}\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{2}^{abc}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{3}^{abc}\text{Re}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{4}^{abc}\text{Im}(\tilde{S}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\right]\\
&+\dot{k}^{\nu}\dot{k}^{\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{k}abc}f^{a}m^{ab}\left[\mathcal{Z}_{5}^{abc}\text{Re}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})+\mathcal{Z}_{6}^{abc}\text{Im}(\tilde{A}_{\nu\mu\rho}^{abc})\right],\end{split}

(2.44)

where
n_{2}^{a}=-\tau\dot{k}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}n_{1}^{a}
yields the nonlinear Drude weight and the various renormalization functions that appear are given by

\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega}^{ab}=1+\dot{k}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}m^{ab}\frac{2\eta^{ab}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\dot{k}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\ln(m^{ab})\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\frac{1+3(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\bigg\},

(2.45)

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{g}^{ab}=&2\eta^{ab}-\dot{k}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}m^{ab}\frac{1-(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\bigg\}\dot{k}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}+\frac{1}{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\left[m^{ab},\dot{k}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\right]\\
&+\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\dot{k}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\ln(m^{ab})\frac{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\bigg\},\end{split}

(2.46)

\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{ab}=-\frac{1}{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\frac{1+2(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{1}{m^{ab}}\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\left[\frac{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}-\frac{2}{(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\frac{1+2(\eta^{ab})^{2}}{1+(\eta^{ab})^{2}}\right]\bigg\},

(2.47)

\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}^{ab}=\mathcal{Z}_{K}^{ab}=1+\frac{1}{2m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\bigg\}

(2.48)

\mathcal{Z}_{C}^{ab}=\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}+\frac{1}{2m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\frac{1}{\eta^{ab}}\bigg\}

(2.49)

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{abc}=\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}-\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}+\frac{1}{2m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}-\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}\right)\bigg\},

(2.50)

\mathcal{Z}_{2}^{abc}=\frac{\eta^{ab}}{\eta^{bc}}-\frac{\eta^{ab}}{\eta^{ca}}+\frac{1}{2m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\left(\frac{\eta^{ab}}{\eta^{bc}}-\frac{\eta^{ab}}{\eta^{ca}}\right)\bigg\},

(2.51)

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{3}^{abc}=&\frac{2}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}\right]\\
&-\frac{1}{m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\left[\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}\right]\bigg\},\end{split}

(2.52)

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{4}^{abc}=&\frac{2}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}\right]\\
&+\frac{1}{m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\left[\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}\right]\bigg\},\end{split}

(2.53)

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{5}^{abc}=&\frac{2}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&+\frac{1}{m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{1}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\bigg\},\end{split}

(2.54)

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{6}^{abc}=&-\frac{2}{\eta^{ab}}\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{\eta^{ab}-\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\\
&+\frac{1}{m^{ab}}\bigg\{\partial^{\sigma},\frac{v_{\sigma}^{a}}{(\bm{v}^{a})^{2}}\left[\frac{\eta^{bc}}{\eta^{ca}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{bc}}{1+(\eta^{bc})^{2}}+\frac{\eta^{ca}}{\eta^{bc}}\frac{1+\eta^{ab}\eta^{ca}}{1+(\eta^{ca})^{2}}\right]\bigg\}.\end{split}

(2.55)

From these, we observe that the distribution derivative terms in the response functions not only affect the carrier densities, but can also be thought of as inducing gradient corrections to the various geometric structures that appear in the equation of motion through the renormalization functions. Comparing Eq. (
2.44
) with the generic relation for the current density,
\sum_{\mathbf{k}a}n^{a}\dot{x}_{\mu}^{a}
, where
n^{a}=f^{a}+n_{1}^{a}+n_{2}^{a}
, the dressed equation of motion of the carrier position is obtained.

2.6
Derivation of Dissipative field equations

To derive the dissipative field equations, Eq. (17) in the main text, it is helpful to recall the expression for the Levi-Civita connection of an arbitrary rank
(p,q)
tensor
\mathcal{O}

194
]
